=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums. Your task is to remove all elements from the array by performing one of the following operations at each step until nums is empty:

Choose any two elements from the first three elements of nums and remove them. The cost of this operation is the maximum of the two elements removed.
If fewer than three elements remain in nums, remove all the remaining elements in a single operation. The cost of this operation is the maximum of the remaining elements.

Return the minimum cost required to remove all the elements.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [6,2,8,4]
Output: 12
Explanation:
Initially, nums = [6, 2, 8, 4].

In the first operation, remove nums[0] = 6 and nums[2] = 8 with a cost of max(6, 8) = 8. Now, nums = [2, 4].
In the second operation, remove the remaining elements with a cost of max(2, 4) = 4.

The cost to remove all elements is 8 + 4 = 12. This is the minimum cost to remove all elements in nums. Hence, the output is 12.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [2,1,3,3]
Output: 5
Explanation:
Initially, nums = [2, 1, 3, 3].

In the first operation, remove nums[0] = 2 and nums[1] = 1 with a cost of max(2, 1) = 2. Now, nums = [3, 3].
In the second operation remove the remaining elements with a cost of max(3, 3) = 3.

The cost to remove all elements is 2 + 3 = 5. This is the minimum cost to remove all elements in nums. Hence, the output is 5.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 1000
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

We have array nums. At each step until empty: if length >=3, choose any two elements from first three elements of nums and remove them. Cost max of two removed. If fewer than three remain, remove all remaining in single operation cost max of remaining. Need min total cost.

Important: Removing elements changes array, "first three elements" are current front three (positions 0,1,2). We can choose any two among them, not necessarily adjacent? Let's formalize.

We have a queue/list. At each operation, if at least 3 elements remain, we can remove two of first three. Which two? Cases:
- Remove positions 0 and 1: then original element at position 2 becomes first.
- Remove positions 0 and 2: then original element at position 1 becomes first.
- Remove positions 1 and 2: then original element at position 0 becomes first.
So after operation, exactly one of the first three original elements remains at front, and the rest of the list follows. Cost is max of the two removed. Thus each operation "advances" through array, keeping one of the first three as survivor to front, discarding two, until fewer than 3 remain; then all remaining (1 or 2 elements) removed at cost max among them.

Need import List? Starter code has List but no import. In LeetCode, typing.List maybe available? We can include `from typing import List`? Usually in code block for LeetCode, they include class Solution, method. We can include import. The prompt says use starter code. We can output:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        INF = 10**18
        dp = [[INF] * (n + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
        # base
        for i in range(n):
            dp[i][n] = nums[i]
        for i in range(n - 1):
            dp[i][n - 1] = max(nums[i], nums[n - 1])
        # But if n=1, n-1=0, range(n-1)=range(0) no issue; dp[0][1] set by first loop? dp[0][n] = nums[0]. For n=1, n=1, dp[0][1]=nums[0]. Good. For n=2, dp[0][2]=nums[0]?             # But if n-2 < 1, range empty. For n=1,2 no recurrence.
            for i in range(j):
                a = nums[i]
                b = nums[j]
                c = nums[j + 1]  # j <= n-2 so j+1 <= n-1 valid
                # keep i
                v1 = max(b, c) + dp[i][j + 2]
                # keep j
                v2 = max(a, c) + dp[j][j + 2]
                # keep j+1
                v3 = max(a, b) + dp[j + 1][j + 2]
                dp[i][j] = min(v1, v2, v3)
        return dp[0][1]

Now, let's consider if there is a way to make code more concise:
```
class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        dp = [[0]*(n+1) for _ in range(n+1)]
        for i in range(n):
            dp[i][n] = nums[i]
        if n > 1:
            for i in range(n-1):
                dp[i][n-1] = max(nums[i], nums[n-1])
        for j in range(n-2, 0, -1):
            for i in range(j):
                a,b,c = nums[i], nums[j], nums[j+1]
                dp[i][j] = min(
                    max(b,c) + dp[i][j+2],
                    max(a,c) + dp[j][j+2],
                    max(a,b) + dp[j+1][j+2]
                )
        return dp[0][1]
```

If n=2, first loop dp[0][2]=nums0, dp[1][2]=nums1; if n>1 loop i=0 dp[0][1]=max(nums0,nums1). Return. Good.
If n=3, first loop dp[0][3], dp[1][3], dp[2][3]; if n>1 loop i=0,1 dp[0][2], dp[1][2]; recurrence j=1. Good.
Using 0 for uncomputed states maybe not an issue because recurrence only accesses computed/base. But to be safe and clear, use INF. However, if we use INF, need set base for j=n-1 and j=n. For n=1, dp[0][1] set. For n=2, dp[0][1] set. Good. Uncomputed states remain INF but not accessed. We'll use INF to avoid any accidental zero if bug. - Base for terminal j = n or n-1. We need `dp_next` for j+2 = n? Actually start with j = n-2 (if n>=3). The recurrence for j=n-2 needs dp at n (base). For j=n-3, recurrence needs dp at n-1 (base), not dp at n-2. If we process every j, the "next" j+2 changes by 1, so we need dp for both n and n-1 bases. But if we only process reachable j (same parity as n?), we can avoid. Since j increases by 2, reachable j values are 1,3,5,... up to final. We don't need unreachable states. We can process only reachable j. That reduces states and memory. Let's explore.

Reachable next index j after t pair operations: j = 1 + 2t, until final. For n, final j = n if n odd? Let's derive:
Starting length n. Pair operations while length >=3. Number of pair operations m = (n-1)//2? For n=4, m=1, final j=3 = n-1. Formula (n-1)//2 =1. n=5, m=2, final j=5=n. Formula 2. n=6, m=2, final j=5=n-1. Formula 2. So final j = 1 + 2m = 1 + 2*((n-1)//2). If n odd, n=2k+1, m=k, final j=2k+1=n. If n even, n=2k, m=k-1? (n-1)//2 = k-1? For n=6, (5)//2=2=k-1, final j=1+4=5=n-1. Yes. So reachable j values: 1,3,..., final_j where final_j = n if n odd else n-1. All odd. We can process j from final_j-2 down to 1 step -2? Actually recurrence from j uses j+2, so we need dp at j+2. If we process descending reachable j: start with base at final_j (terminal), then j = final_j - 2, etc., down to 1. For n=5, final_j=5 base; process j=3, then j=1. For n=6, final_j=5 base; process j=3, then j=1. Wait n=6 reachable j after pair ops: initial j=1, after first op j=3, after second op j=5 final. So process j=3 then 1. Yes. For n=4, final_j=3 base; process j=1. For n=3, final_j=3 base; process j=1. For n=2, final_j=1 base (terminal, no pair ops). For n=1, final_j=1 base. So reachable j are odd from 1 to final_j. We can process j = final_j - 2 down to 1 step -2. This avoids unreachable states and reduces memory.

Now, O(n) memory with reachable j:
We need maintain `dp_next` = list of dp[i][j+2] for i in range(j+2?) Actually for current j, `j+2` is the next reachable index (already computed). We need entries for i up to j+1. Then loop:
```
j = final_j - 2
while j >= 1:
    cur_dp = [0]*j
    for i in range(j):
        a = nums[i]; b = nums[j]; c = nums[j+1]
        cur_dp[i] = min(
            max(b,c) + next_dp[i],
            max(a,c) + next_dp[j],
            max(a,b) + next_dp[j+1]
        )
    next_dp = cur_dp
    j -= 2
return next_dp[0]  # after loop j=1, next_dp length 1 = dp[0][1]
```
Wait after computing j=1, cur_dp length 1, set next_dp = cur_dp, return next_dp[0]. Good.

This O(n) memory version is elegant and avoids 2D. Let's verify recurrence with O(n) for all cases. It processes only reachable j (odd). But is it valid to ignore unreachable states? O(n) memory code:
```
class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        if n % 2 == 1:
            final_j = n
            dp_next = nums[:]  # dp[i][n] = nums[i]
        else:
            final_j = n - 1
            dp_next = [max(nums[i], nums[-1]) for i in range(n - 1)]
        j = final_j - 2
        while j >= 1:
            cur = [0] * j
            b = nums[j]
            c = nums[j + 1]
            for i in range(j):
                a = nums[i]
                cur[i] = min(
                    max(b, c) + dp_next[i],
                    max(a, c) + dp_next[j],
                    max(a, b) + dp_next[j + 1]
                )
            dp_next = cur
            j -= 2
        return dp_next[0]
```
Need check n=1: n odd, final_j=1, dp_next=nums[:] length1. j=-1, while false, return dp_next[0]. Good.
n=2: n even, final_j=1, dp_next=[max(nums[0], nums[1])], j=-1, return. Good.
n=3: n odd, final_j=3, dp_next=nums[:] length3. j=1, b=nums[1], c=nums[2]. i=0. dp_next[j]=dp_next[1], dp_next[j+1]=dp_next[2]. Good.
n=4: n even, final_j=3, dp_next length3. j=1, b=nums[1], c=nums[2]. dp_next[j]=dp_next[1], dp_next[j+1]=dp_next[2]. Good. Note dp_next[1] = max(nums[1], nums[3]), dp_next[2] = max(nums[2], nums[3]). Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any possible issue with using `max(a, c)` and `max(a, b)` inside loop; if a is large, okay. We can precompute `bc` but not necessary. Let's write final code with comments maybe:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        # dp_next[i] = dp[i][j+2] for current j
        if n % 2:
            dp_next = nums[:]
        else:
            dp_next = [max(nums[i], nums[-1]) for i in range(n - 1)]

        j = (n if n % 2 else n - 1) - 2
        while j >= 1:
            b = nums[j]
            c = nums[j + 1]
            bc = max(b, c)
            cur = [0] * j
            for i in range(j):
                a = nums[i]
                cur[i] = min(
                    bc + dp_next[i],
                    max(a, c) + dp_next[j],
                    max(a, b) + dp_next[j + 1]
                )
            dp_next = cur
            j -= 2
        return dp_next[0]
```
This is concise. But note `j = (n if n % 2 else n - 1) - 2`; for n=1, j=-1; while false. For n=2, j=-1. Good.

For n even, dp_next length n-1. j = n-3 (since final_j=n-1, j=final_j-2=n-3). dp_next[j]=dp_next[n-3], dp_next[j+1]=dp_next[n-2], both valid length n-1. For n=4, j=1, dp_next length3, dp_next[1], dp_next[2]. Good. For n=2, no loop. Good.

Our `cur` includes all. Good.
But what about `dp_next[i]` for i between j' and current_j-1? They are used as `dp_next[j']` and `dp_next[j'+1]` only, not for i range. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any possible issue with the code if the array length is 1000 and values are all 1e6, dp_next base for even = [1e6]*
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
>
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)

        # dp_next[i] represents dp[i][j+2] for the current j.
        if n % 2 == 1:
            # Terminal state: only nums[i] remains.
            dp_next = nums[:]
            j = n - 2
        else:
            # Terminal state: nums[i] and nums[-1] remain.
            dp_next = [max(nums[i], nums[-1]) for i in range(n - 1)]
            j = n - 3

        while j >= 1:
            b = nums[j]
            c = nums[j + 1]
            bc = max(b, c)
            cur = [0] * j

            for i in range(j):
                a = nums[i]
                cur[i] = min(
                    bc + dp_next[i],          # keep nums[i]
                    max(a, c) + dp_next[j],   # keep nums[j]
                    max(a, b) + dp_next[j + 1] # keep nums[j + 1]
                )

            dp_next = cur
            j -= 2

        return dp_next[0]
```
